<a id="sec:introduction"></a>
### Introduction

Vertex operator algebras and conformal nets are two mathematical formulations of chiral conformal field theory. A vertex operator algebra records field modes and their algebraic identities. A conformal net assigns von Neumann algebras of bounded observables to intervals of the circle. Their expected correspondence has both an analytic and a representation-theoretic part. Formal commutation of fields must lead to commuting local algebras, and algebraic modules must correspond to Hilbert-space sectors with the same fusion and braiding.

We establish this correspondence for every simple unitary strongly rational complex vertex operator algebra. The functor in the result is the Carpi–Weiner–Xu construction: it completes a unitary module and integrates its actual closed smeared fields. The tensor product on the net side is Connes fusion, with the Doplicher–Haag–Roberts braiding.

<a id="pm-h-3"></a>
#### The statement

Let $V$ be a complex vertex operator algebra, with vacuum $\Omega$, conformal vector $\nu$ and conformal energy $L_0$. It is of *CFT type* if $$V=\bigoplus_{n\geq0}V_n,\qquad V_0=\mathbb C\Omega.$$ Write $C_2(V)=\operatorname{span}\{a_{(-2)}b:a,b\in V\}$. We call $V$ *strongly rational* if it is of CFT type, is isomorphic to its graded contragredient, is rational, and has finite-dimensional quotient $V/C_2(V)$. Here rationality means complete reducibility of admissible modules. Unitarity means that $V$ has a normalized positive invariant Hermitian form and its corresponding antilinear PCT involution $\Theta$.

We use grading-restricted finite-length modules: their conformal-energy spaces are finite dimensional, and their weights are bounded below. Their algebraic braided tensor category is denoted by $\mathop{\mathrm{Rep}}(V)$. Its tensor product is the Huang–Lepowsky fusion product. The category $\mathop{\mathrm{Rep}}^u(V)$ consists of such modules with positive invariant Hermitian forms, and module homomorphisms. We call $V$ *strongly unitary* when every simple module is unitarizable, and *completely unitary* when, in addition, the canonical invariant Hermitian fusion forms are positive definite. In the latter case the module category is a unitary modular tensor category.

<a id="eq:intro-net"></a>
For a homogeneous $a\in V$ of weight $d_a$, write $$Y(a,z)=\sum_{n\in\mathbb Z}a_{(n)}z^{-n-1}.$$ Polynomial energy bounds mean that, for each $a$, there are $C>0$ and nonnegative integers $p,k$ such that <a id="eq:intro-energy"></a>$$\label{eq:intro-energy}
 \|a_{(n)}b\|\leq C(1+|n|)^p\|(1+L_0)^k b\|,
 \qquad n\in\mathbb Z,\quad b\in V.$$ These bounds define closable smeared fields <a id="eq:intro-smear"></a>$$\label{eq:intro-smear}
 Y(a,f)=\oint_{S^1}Y(a,z)f(z)z^{d_a}\frac{dz}{2\pi i z},
 \qquad f\in C^\infty(S^1),$$ initially on $V$. For a nonempty nondense open interval $I\subset S^1$, set $$\label{eq:intro-net}
 A_V(I)=W^*\!\left\{\overline{Y(a,f)}:
 a\in V\text{ homogeneous},\ \mathop{\mathrm{supp}}f\subset I\right\}.$$ Here the von Neumann algebra generated by closed operators is the algebra generated by their bounded graph or polar data. The VOA is *CKLW-strongly local* if it is energy-bounded and $A_V(I)\subset A_V(I')'$, where $I'$ is the interior of the complementary arc.

A conformal net $A$ is *completely rational* if it is irreducible, split, strongly additive and has finite $\mu$-index. Split means that separated interval algebras generate their spatial tensor product. Strong additivity means that removing one interior point from an interval does not change the algebra generated by its two components. For two disjoint intervals $E=I_1\cup I_3$, the $\mu$-index is the index of $A(E)\subset A(E')'$. We write $\mathop{\mathrm{Rep}}^f(A)$ for the additive category of finite-index, equivalently dualizable, locally normal DHR representations, including finite sums of the tensor unit. The scalar case is described in Section <a href="#sec:preliminaries" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:preliminaries">2</a>.

<a id="eq:intro-cwx"></a>
A unitary $V$-module $M$ is *strongly integrable* if there are compatible normal representations $\pi_I^M$ of $A_V(I)$ on its Hilbert completion $\mathcal H_M$ satisfying $$\label{eq:intro-cwx}
 \pi_I^M\!\left(\overline{Y^V(a,f)}\right)
   =\overline{Y^M(a,f)},\qquad \mathop{\mathrm{supp}}f\subset I.$$ The equality includes the domains of the closed operators. Normal representations act on affiliated operators through their graph projections. The resulting Hilbert-completion functor is denoted by $F_{\mathrm{CWX}}^V$.

<a id="stmt-thm-main"></a>
<div id="thm:main" class="theorem">

**Theorem 1**. *Let $V$ be a simple unitary strongly rational vertex operator algebra over $\mathbb C$. Then:*

1.  *$V$ has polynomial energy bounds and is CKLW-strongly local. The algebras <a href="#eq:intro-net" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:intro-net">[eq:intro-net]</a> form an irreducible conformal net $A_V$.*

2.  *$V$ is completely unitary: every simple grading-restricted module is unitarizable, and all canonical invariant Hermitian fusion forms are positive definite.*

3.  *Every unitary grading-restricted finite-length module is strongly integrable, with the closed-field equality <a href="#eq:intro-cwx" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:intro-cwx">[eq:intro-cwx]</a>.*

4.  *The net $A_V$ is completely rational.*

5.  *After choosing invariant positive forms on the simple modules, the Carpi–Weiner–Xu functor gives a braided unitary tensor equivalence $$\mathop{\mathrm{Rep}}(V)\simeq\mathop{\mathrm{Rep}}^u(V)
     \xrightarrow{\ F_{\mathrm{CWX}}^V\ }\mathop{\mathrm{Rep}}^f(A_V).$$ The tensor structure and braiding on the right are Connes fusion and DHR braiding, with a common orientation convention for the circle.*

</div>

The equivalence also compares extensions of the two theories. A conformal VOA extension enlarges $V$ while preserving its vacuum and conformal vector; a local net extension enlarges the interval algebras compatibly with their conformal symmetries. Gui’s comparison theorems identify their finite-index counterparts through the corresponding commutative algebra objects. Corollary <a href="#cor:finite-index-extensions" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cor:finite-index-extensions">22</a> gives the precise normalized correspondence and identifies the representation functors of the extended theories.

<a id="pm-h-4"></a>
#### The correspondence problem and earlier work

Carpi, Kawahigashi, Longo and Weiner made strong locality the analytic condition for constructing a conformal net from a simple unitary VOA <a id="pm-cite-1"></a>[[CKLW](#ref-CKLW), Theorems 6.8 and 8.1]. Their examples include unitary affine and Virasoro VOAs and the moonshine VOA, and their construction is compatible with unitary subalgebras. They conjectured strong locality for every simple unitary VOA . Theorem <a href="#thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main">1</a> establishes this assertion under strong rationality and then compares the full representation theories in that setting. The locality issue is substantial: commuting unbounded fields on a common domain need not generate commuting von Neumann algebras.

The two representation theories already possess rich tensor structures. Huang’s results give rigidity and modularity for strongly rational VOAs <a id="pm-cite-2"></a>[[HuangTensor](#ref-HuangTensor), [HuangRigidity](#ref-HuangRigidity)], while Kawahigashi–Longo–Müger establish modularity for completely rational conformal nets <a id="pm-cite-3"></a>[[KLM](#ref-KLM)]. Identifying these structures requires more than constructing the vacuum net. The Carpi–Weiner–Xu functor integrates unitary modules through their closed fields and is fully faithful on strongly integrable modules <a id="pm-cite-4"></a>[[GuiUnitaryII](#ref-GuiUnitaryII), Theorem 4.3], <a id="pm-cite-5"></a>[[GuiExtensions](#ref-GuiExtensions), Theorem 4.24]. Gui’s tensor comparison then identifies VOA fusion and braiding with Connes fusion and DHR braiding under charged-field hypotheses <a id="pm-cite-6"></a>[[GuiUnbounded](#ref-GuiUnbounded), Corollary 3.23].

Complete unitarity, full braided equivalence and complete rationality are already known for affine VOAs at positive integral levels, even positive-definite lattice VOAs, parafermion VOAs at positive integral levels, and type $ADE$ discrete-series $W$-algebras. Gui’s results also cover finite tensor products of these classes and regular cosets of unitary inclusions between such tensor products <a id="pm-cite-7"></a>[[GuiUnbounded](#ref-GuiUnbounded), Theorem I]. His extension results propagate the comparison through further extensions and coset constructions under their stated hypotheses <a id="pm-cite-8"></a>[[GuiExtensions](#ref-GuiExtensions), Theorem 6.18 and Example 6.19]. Here the analytic and positivity hypotheses for the tensor embedding are established from strong rationality and unitarity, and a separate reconstruction argument proves that every finite-index sector is in its image.

In the converse direction, Henriques and Tener construct a unitary VOA from a conformal net with finite-dimensional vacuum energy spaces, recover a VOA from its associated net under their AQFT-locality condition, and reconstruct modules from net representations <a id="pm-cite-9"></a>[[HTVOA](#ref-HTVOA), Theorems A–C and 8.9]. Their AQFT-locality condition does not include polynomial energy bounds. Their annulus representation theory and bounded vacuum operations for multidisc embeddings provide the geometric tools used here <a id="pm-cite-10"></a>[[HTVirasoro](#ref-HTVirasoro), [HTGenusZero](#ref-HTGenusZero)]. Those constructions start from an existing net. In this paper they enter after the independent proof of strong locality, first for creation fields and ultimately for the reconstruction of arbitrary irreducible sectors.

Two earlier analytic methods are especially important to the proof. Carpi, Tanimoto and Weiner showed that optimal energy order for Hermitian primary generators implies strong locality <a id="pm-cite-11"></a>[[CTW](#ref-CTW), Theorem 3.7], with the correction <a id="pm-cite-12"></a>[[CTWCorrection](#ref-CTWCorrection)]. Carpi and Tomassini obtained a $C_2$ exponential bound and used a contracting estimate to improve exponential growth to polynomial growth <a id="pm-cite-13"></a>[[CarpiTomassini](#ref-CarpiTomassini), Proposition 3.17 and Theorem 4.5]. Here a finite-$C_2$ filtration supplies the first contraction for a generating family. A sharper normal-product count and a second contraction then yield the optimal order required for locality.

For loop-group models based on $SU(N)$, Wassermann computed Connes fusion using bounded primary fields and their transport relations <a id="pm-cite-14"></a>[[Wassermann](#ref-Wassermann)]. Gui’s transport identities and Tener’s transport-form framework express candidate VOA fusion inner products through ordinary vertex operators <a id="pm-cite-15"></a>[[GuiUnitaryII](#ref-GuiUnitaryII), [TenerFusion](#ref-TenerFusion)]; Gui further developed their canonical normalization and geometric positivity <a id="pm-cite-16"></a>[[GuiPositivity](#ref-GuiPositivity)]. Tener’s positivity theorem for regular VOAs assumes a unitary structure on the algebraic fusion product and a corresponding net representation of the source module <a id="pm-cite-17"></a>[[TenerFusion](#ref-TenerFusion), Theorem 4.4]. Our argument constructs positivity on the full algebraic fusion object before presuming that it is unitarizable. To reach the remaining simple modules, we use Carnahan–Miyamoto’s fixed-point regularity and the Barron–Dong–Mason permutation construction <a id="pm-cite-18"></a>[[CarnahanMiyamoto](#ref-CarnahanMiyamoto), [BDM](#ref-BDM)]. The required permutation product rests on the extension tensor structures of Creutzig–Kanade–McRae and McRae <a id="pm-cite-19"></a>[[CKMExtensions](#ref-CKMExtensions), [McRaeTwisted](#ref-McRaeTwisted)] and the one-slot intertwiner correspondence of Dong–Li–Xu–Yu <a id="pm-cite-20"></a>[[DLXY](#ref-DLXY), Theorems 5.5 and 5.9].

<a id="pm-h-5"></a>
#### Proof strategy

The central ordering issue is that a positive form on $V$ does not initially give positive forms on its modules or fusion products. We first work with modules already carrying such forms. An intertwining operator is viewed as a charged field, with a charge module, a source module and a target module. Its creation case has the vacuum $V$ as source. This supplies a starting point for the following sequence.

1.  *From $C_2$-cofiniteness to a net.* In Section <a href="#sec:energy" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:energy">3</a>, a finite generating family controls all normal products, and positivity of certain zero modes gives an exponential starting bound. Two contraction inequalities first give polynomial growth and then, for Hermitian quasi-primary fields of weight $d\geq2$, the energy order $d-1$ needed for locality. A sharper normal-product count and a Möbius estimate supply the second contraction.

2.  *Integrating modules with given positive forms.* In Section <a href="#sec:annuli" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:annuli">4</a>, a fixed doubled annulus bounds local insertions by the norm of the inserted state. A Möbius factorization makes a round-annulus estimate uniform as the insertion approaches the circle. These estimates give polynomial bounds and bounded local regularizations for creation fields. Section <a href="#sec:integration" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:integration">5</a> uses their total ranges to construct normal local-algebra actions on every already unitary module, with equality of the actual closed smeared fields.

3.  *Positive fusion for unitary inputs.* Creation operators give positive Gram matrices in the vacuum local algebra. The normal representations just constructed transfer those matrices to any unitary source module. In Section <a href="#sec:fusion" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:fusion">6</a>, rigidity supplies a nondegenerate invariant form on the algebraic fusion product, and positivity of polynomial moments separates its conformal-energy spaces. This proves positivity of the canonical fusion form. The resulting charged-field bounds then apply to every intertwining operator between unitary modules.

4.  *Unitarizing all simple modules.* Fix a simple module $P$, with graded contragredient $P^\vee$, set $D=V\otimes V$, and let $U$ be its flip-fixed subalgebra. The transposition-twisted $D$-module $T$ obtained from the vacuum $V$ is unitary over $U$. In Section <a href="#sec:unitarity" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:unitarity">7</a>, the permutation product detects every simple constituent of $R=P\otimes P^\vee$, restricted to $U$, inside the positive fusion product $T\boxtimes_U T^\vee$. Thus $R$ has a positive $U$-invariant form. The action of the anti-invariant part of $D$ produces a second such form; their sum is invariant under all of $D$. Restriction to a copy of $P$ gives the required positive $V$-invariant form.

5.  *From a tensor embedding to all sectors.* Section <a href="#sec:comparison" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:comparison">8</a> now verifies Gui’s hypotheses for the CWX braided unitary tensor embedding, so every module gives a finite-index sector. Section <a href="#sec:exhaustion" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:exhaustion">9</a> starts with an arbitrary irreducible locally normal net representation, without an index or finite-energy-multiplicity assumption. Its rotation spectrum is discrete. Henriques–Tener reconstruction, including <a id="pm-cite-21"></a>[[HTVOA](#ref-HTVOA), Remark 9.8], and algebraic regularity produce an ordinary simple module inside it; a reducing-projection argument identifies the whole sector with that module. The finite list of sectors, the split property and the Longo–Xu dichotomy give complete rationality. Decomposition into irreducibles then proves the equivalence for all additive finite-index objects.

Optimal energy order is needed for ordinary fields to generate a local net. For charged fields, polynomial bounds combined with bounded annulus regularization suffice.

<a id="sec:preliminaries"></a>
### Algebraic and analytic conventions

Throughout, $V$ has the hypotheses of Theorem <a href="#thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main">1</a>. We collect the algebraic inputs that do not require positivity of all modules, and fix the operator conventions used in the estimates.

<a id="pm-h-7"></a>
#### Modules, duality and fusion

By the regularity theorem of Abe–Buhl–Dong <a id="pm-cite-22"></a>[[ABD](#ref-ABD), Theorem 4.5], every weak $V$-module is an algebraic direct sum of ordinary simple modules. There are finitely many simple isomorphism classes. Their lowest conformal weights are rational <a id="pm-cite-23"></a>[[DLMModularInvariance](#ref-DLMModularInvariance), Theorem 11.3]. These weights are not assumed nonnegative before a positive invariant form is known. In the grading-restricted category, each simple occurs with finite multiplicity: infinitely many copies would give an infinite-dimensional space at its lowest weight. Thus the categories used here have finite length.

The Huang–Lepowsky tensor theory supplies the fusion product $M\boxtimes N$ and its universal intertwining operator. For a target $K$, postcomposition identifies $$\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}_V(M\boxtimes N,K)
 \quad\text{with intertwining operators of type}\quad
 \binom{K}{M\ N}.$$ Products and iterates of these operators have absolutely convergent matrix coefficients in their respective regions, and the fusing isomorphism identifies them. We use the positive-real convention $$x_1>x_2>x_1-x_2>0$$ for this identification, with real logarithms. The convergence and associativity statements include sums over intermediate conformal-energy projections <a id="pm-cite-24"></a>[[HuangTensor](#ref-HuangTensor), Theorems 3.5 and 3.7, Remark 3.8]. Huang’s rigidity and modularity theorems apply under precisely the present strong rationality hypotheses <a id="pm-cite-25"></a>[[HuangRigidity](#ref-HuangRigidity), Theorems 1.1, 3.8 and 4.6]. In particular the graded contragredient $M^\vee$ is a categorical dual. No positive form on $M$ is needed for these algebraic assertions.

We use ordinary, nonlogarithmic intertwining operators; the modules under consideration are semisimple with diagonalizable $L_0$. Their skew-symmetry and contragredient transformations are those of Frenkel–Huang–Lepowsky <a id="pm-cite-26"></a>[[FHL](#ref-FHL)]. The conjugate module $\bar M$ is the conjugate vector space with the action conjugated using $\Theta$. For an arbitrary module its *Hermitian dual* is $$M^{\mathrm h}=(\bar M)^\vee.$$ The natural pairing is <a id="eq:hermitian-pairing"></a>$$\label{eq:hermitian-pairing}
 [x,\lambda]_M=\overline{\lambda(\bar x)},
 \qquad x\in M,\quad\lambda\in M^{\mathrm h}.$$ It is linear in $x$ and conjugate-linear in $\lambda$. This pairing has no positivity assertion. An invariant sesquilinear form on $M$ corresponds to a module map $M\to M^{\mathrm h}$.

All Hermitian inner products in the paper are linear in their first variable. A unitary module is a finite orthogonal sum of unitary simples: the grade-wise orthogonal complement of a submodule is invariant by the mode adjoint identities. A homomorphism between finite-length unitary modules extends to a bounded operator on their completions, and its adjoint is again a module homomorphism. To see the boundedness, decompose into simple types. On an irreducible type invariant positive forms differ by a positive scalar, by Schur’s lemma; the remaining maps act on finite-dimensional multiplicity spaces.

The spectrum of $L_0$ on a unitary module is nonnegative. At a lowest vector of weight $h$, the relation $[L_1,L_{-1}]=2L_0$ gives $\|L_{-1}x\|^2=2h\|x\|^2$. Every weight in that simple module is $h+n$ for an integer $n\geq0$; finite orthogonal sums give the general case. Constants in our estimates may depend on the fixed module; uniformity over all modules is unnecessary.

<a id="pm-h-8"></a>
#### Modes, smooth vectors and closed operators

For a homogeneous state $a$ of weight $d$, the *physical modes* are <a id="eq:physical-modes"></a>$$\label{eq:physical-modes}
 a[m]=a_{(m+d-1)},\qquad
 [L_0,a[m]]=-m\,a[m].$$ We use the same convention for intertwining fields, whose physical indices may lie in nonintegral translates of $\mathbb Z$. On an algebraic module, let $\Pi_\lambda$ denote the projection onto the $L_0$-eigenspace of energy $\lambda$ along the other eigenspaces. This definition does not require a Hermitian form. On a unitary completion, the same symbol denotes its orthogonal extension, and $P_N$ denotes the spectral projection onto energies at most $N$. We write $$\mathcal H_M^\infty
 =\bigcap_{k\geq0}\mathop{\mathrm{Dom}}(1+L_0)^k$$ for the smooth vectors, with their energy Sobolev seminorms. Their finite-energy subspace is the algebraic module $M$.

<a id="eq:hermitian-mode-adjoint"></a>
A state is *quasi-primary* if $L_1a=0$, and *primary* if $L_na=0$ for every $n>0$. A quasi-primary state of integral weight $d$ is called Hermitian when $(-1)^d\Theta a=a$. The invariant form then gives the level-wise adjoint identity $$\label{eq:hermitian-mode-adjoint}
 a[m]^*=a[-m].$$ For a general state, the adjoint formula has the additional, finitely many $L_1$-descendant terms. We use <a href="#eq:hermitian-mode-adjoint" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:hermitian-mode-adjoint">[eq:hermitian-mode-adjoint]</a> only with its stated hypothesis.

Polynomial mode bounds imply that smooth smearings and their formal adjoints map smooth vectors continuously to smooth vectors. Indeed Fourier coefficients of a smooth test decay faster than every power, and commuting $(1+L_0)^k$ across a mode costs only a polynomial in its energy shift. Finite-energy truncations converge in all these seminorms. Consequently the closures of a polynomially bounded smear from finite-energy vectors and from smooth vectors agree. We will repeatedly use this explicit core.

For a charged field, smearing is performed on an interval with a chosen angular lift; its physical normalization is $z^d\mathcal Y(a,z)$. Fractional powers use the branch specified by that lift. Changing the normalization by a smooth nowhere-zero phase merely changes the test function. At a reflected coordinate the conjugate branch is always used.

Positive-energy unitary Virasoro representations integrate to the centrally extended covering group of circle diffeomorphisms and satisfy the usual linear energy bounds <a id="pm-cite-27"></a>[[CKLW](#ref-CKLW), Proposition 3.2, Remark 3.3 and Theorem 3.4]; see also <a id="pm-cite-28"></a>[[ToledanoLaredo](#ref-ToledanoLaredo), Theorem 5.2.1 and Proposition 5.3.1]. The resulting Möbius and diffeomorphism operators preserve the smooth space. We use their fixed conformal normalization; in particular the Virasoro relation $[L_1,L_{-1}]=2L_0$ fixes the additive constant in $L_0$. Annulus actions and their Sobolev properties will be introduced only after the local net has been constructed.

For a closed densely defined operator $T$, its graph is a closed subspace of the direct sum of its source and target Hilbert spaces. The entries of the orthogonal graph projection are bounded operators. Graph projections allow us to state precisely that a normal representation carries one closed field to another, or that a bounded projection reduces a closed field. Agreement on a dense set alone will not be used as a substitute for agreement of closed graphs.

<a id="pm-h-9"></a>
#### Creation and the adjoint convention

<a id="eq:creation"></a>
<a id="eq:theta-convention"></a>
For a unitary module $M$, the creation intertwining operator is $$\label{eq:creation}
 \mathcal Y_M^+(a,z)v=e^{zL_{-1}}Y^M(v,-z)a,
 \qquad a\in M,\quad v\in V.$$ Its type is $\binom{M}{M\ V}$. We use Tener’s adjoint convention <a id="pm-cite-29"></a>[[TenerFusion](#ref-TenerFusion), Section 2 and Proposition 3.10]; its adjoint, the annihilation operator, is $$\mathcal Y_M^-=(\mathcal Y_M^+)^\dagger,
 \qquad\text{of type }\binom{V}{\bar M\ M}.$$ For positive $r$, put $$\label{eq:theta-convention}
 \theta_r a=e^{rL_1}e^{i\pi L_0}r^{-2L_0}\bar a\in\bar M.$$ For complex coordinates the logarithms follow the chosen angular lift. The reflection formula uses $\theta_{\bar w}$ and the coordinate $\bar w^{-1}$. These conventions fix the phase of every transport form below, including when the lowest weight of $M$ is nonintegral. The comparison with Gui’s canonical fusion normalization is made explicitly in the positivity argument.

Tener’s Propositions 3.10–3.11 give two inputs we will need before general fusion positivity. First, creation satisfies the vacuum-source transport identity. Second, for unitary charge, source and target, an intertwining operator applied to finite-energy vectors has Hilbert-space values on $0<|z|<1$, holomorphic on each branch. The latter conclusion uses regularity and convergence of products with the adjoint intertwiner. It does not require an energy bound or a net representation on the source. We will distinguish this Hilbert-valued convergence from the operator bounds that we prove later.

<a id="pm-h-10"></a>
#### The scalar case

If $V=\mathbb C\Omega$, the conformal vector is zero, every grading-restricted module is finite dimensional in degree zero, and all fields are constant scalar fields. The net on the one-dimensional vacuum space is the scalar net. It is irreducible, split and strongly additive, and its two-interval index is one. Its additive dualizable representation category is the category of finite-dimensional Hilbert spaces. Creation, fusion and CWX integration are the usual tensor product over $\mathbb C$, with the flip braiding. Thus Theorem <a href="#thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main">1</a> holds in this case.

We assume $V\neq\mathbb C\Omega$ henceforth. Its central charge is positive: $\|\nu\|^2=c/2$, so $c=0$ would force $\nu=0$, and then CFT type would force $V=V_0=\mathbb C\Omega$. The nontriviality assumption is used in the flip-orbifold argument and in the usual conformal-net sector theory.

<a id="sec:energy"></a>
### Energy bounds and strong locality

Our first analytic task is to construct the vacuum net. We do this without assuming that any module has already been integrated to a net representation. The argument has two stages. A finite set of quasi-primary generators first gives polynomial energy bounds of unspecified order. A second estimate improves the order to the conformal weight minus one, which is the order needed for the strong-locality criterion.

<a id="stmt-thm-energy"></a>
<div id="thm:energy" class="theorem">

<a id="eq:optimal-vertex-bound"></a>
**Theorem 2**. *Let $V$ be a simple unitary strongly rational vertex operator algebra, and let $W$ be a grading-restricted unitary $V$-module. Every vertex operator on $W$ satisfies polynomial energy bounds. More precisely, if $a\in V_d$ is quasi-primary, $d\geq2$, and $(-1)^d\Theta a=a$, then there are constants $C_a>0$ and $p_a\in\mathbb Z_{\geq0}$ such that $$\label{eq:optimal-vertex-bound}
 \|a[m]\xi\|
 \leq C_a(1+|m|)^{p_a}
       \|(1+L_0)^{d-1}\xi\|,
 \qquad m\in\mathbb Z,\quad \xi\in W.$$ Weight-one fields and the Virasoro field satisfy energy bounds of at most linear order. The constants may depend on $V$, $W$, and $a$.*

</div>

<a id="eq:energy-adjoint"></a>
The scalar VOA satisfies the theorem directly. We assume for the proof that $V\ne\mathbb C\Omega$, and fix $W$. Since $W$ is a finite orthogonal sum of ordinary unitary modules, it suffices to establish all estimates on those summands and take the maximum of the finitely many constants. Write $W(h)$ for the energy-$h$ subspace, with $W(h)=0$ when $h$ is absent. These spaces are finite dimensional and mutually orthogonal. Unitarity of the positive-energy Virasoro representation gives $h\geq0$. We use the spectral projections $$P_N=\mathbf1_{[0,N]}(L_0),\qquad
 \Pi_h=\mathbf1_{\{h\}}(L_0),\qquad H=1+L_0.$$ All mode identities below initially act on finite-energy vectors. For an Hermitian quasi-primary $a$ of weight $d$, the invariant form gives $$\label{eq:energy-adjoint}
 a[m]^*=a[-m],\qquad
 [L_j,a[m]]=((d-1)j-m)a[j+m]\quad(j=-1,0,1),$$ where the adjoint is taken between the indicated energy spaces.

<a id="pm-h-12"></a>
#### A finite generating filtration

Choose an integer $D\geq2$ such that $V_w=C_2(V)_w$ for all $w>D$. In each weight $1,\ldots,D$, choose a finite set of Hermitian vectors spanning the quasi-primary space, and let $S$ be their union. Such a choice is possible because $(-1)^w\Theta$ is an antilinear involution of that space. We distinguish the conformal weight of a monomial from the sum of the weights of its generators.

<a id="stmt-lem-energy-filtration"></a>
<div id="lem:energy-filtration" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:energy-monomial"></a>
**Lemma 3**. *Every homogeneous vector of weight $w$ is a linear combination of monomials $$\label{eq:energy-monomial}
 b=a^1_{(-1-n_1)}\cdots a^\ell_{(-1-n_\ell)}\Omega,
 \qquad a^i\in S,\quad n_i\geq0,$$ with $$D_b:=\sum_{i=1}^{\ell}d_{a^i},\qquad
 w=D_b+\sum_{i=1}^{\ell}n_i.$$ If the vector belongs to $C_2(V)_w$, the monomials can be chosen with $D_b\leq w-1$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-energy-filtration"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-energy-filtration</summary>

<a id="eq:energy-filtration-stability"></a>
*Proof.* Let $E_t$ be the span of arbitrary-mode strings of elements of $S$ on $\Omega$ whose total generator weight is at most $t$. Induction on string length, using the iterate identity, gives $$\label{eq:energy-filtration-stability}
 u_{(r)}E_s\subset E_{t+s}\qquad(u\in E_t, r\in\mathbb Z).$$ For the induction step, write a chosen string as $u=a_{(m)}u'$, with $a\in S$ and $u'$ of generator degree at most $t-d_a$. The iterate formula expresses $u_{(r)}v$, $v\in E_s$, as a sum of terms of the two forms $$a_{(m-j)}u'_{(r+j)}v,
 \qquad u'_{(m+r-j)}a_{(j)}v,
 \qquad j\geq0.$$ For the first, apply induction to $u'$ and then prepend the generator mode. For the second, $a_{(j)}v\in E_{s+d_a}$, so induction on $u'$ again gives a vector in $E_{t+s}$. Each of the two sums truncates on the fixed vector $v$.

We first prove $V_w\subset E_w$ by induction on $w$. At weights at most $D$, use the orthogonal decomposition $$V_w=(V_w\cap\ker L_1)+L_{-1}V_{w-1}.$$ The first summand is spanned by our chosen generators, and differentiation does not increase generator weight. A homogeneous term $u_{(-2)}v$ of weight $w$ has $\mathrm{wt}(u)+\mathrm{wt}(v)=w-1$. If both factors have positive weight, induction and <a href="#eq:energy-filtration-stability" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:energy-filtration-stability">[eq:energy-filtration-stability]</a> put it in $E_{w-1}$. If $v=\Omega$, it is $L_{-1}u$ and has the same property; terms with $u\in\mathbb C\Omega$ vanish. This proves $C_2(V)_w\subset E_{w-1}$ and completes the induction above $D$ as well.

Now order an arbitrary-mode string by moving nonnegative ordinary modes to the right. A commutator of modes of generators $a,b$ involves $a_{(j)}b$, $j\geq0$, of weight at most $d_a+d_b-1$. The inclusion just proved places this state in $E_{d_a+d_b-1}$. Thus every commutator term lowers the total generator weight, whereas the uncommuted term moves the chosen nonnegative mode one place to the right. Induction on total generator weight, and then on the number of such moves, puts the string into negative ordinary-mode form. A nonnegative mode reaching $\Omega$ vanishes. Taking homogeneous components proves both assertions. ◻

</details>

We shall use only finitely many expansions from Lemma <a href="#lem:energy-filtration" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:energy-filtration">3</a> at a time. Constants may depend on these fixed expansions. In particular, no uniformity over all monomials is asserted.

<a id="eq:energy-left-right-indices"></a>
Here is the mode expansion that controls the intermediate energies. Iterating the negative-product formula from the outside of <a href="#eq:energy-monomial" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:energy-monomial">[eq:energy-monomial]</a> places each generator either on the left, with physical index $$\label{eq:energy-left-right-indices}
 -d_{a^i}-n_i-j_i,
 \qquad\text{or on the right, with index}\qquad
 1-d_{a^i}+j_i,\qquad j_i\geq0.$$ The coefficients are products of signed binomial coefficients, bounded by $C_b\prod_i(1+j_i)^{n_i}$. There are finitely many order patterns; in each pattern every left factor precedes every right factor. The vacuum mode imposes the constraint that the physical indices sum to the desired mode of $b$.

In a matrix element with both endpoint energies at most $N$, move the left factors to the testing vector by adjunction. They then all lower energy. Each right factor can raise energy by at most $d_{a^i}-1$. Every intermediate vector therefore lies in $P_{N+c_b}W$ for a fixed integer $c_b$, and every relevant $j_i$ is $O_b(1+N)$. This proves finiteness of the sums used below and permits the insertion of those fixed intermediate cutoffs.

<a id="pm-h-13"></a>
#### Polynomial energy bounds

<a id="eq:rough-monomial-bound"></a>
For integer $N\geq1$, define $$B_N=\max\left(1,
       \max_{a\in S,\,m\in\mathbb Z}
          \|P_Na[m]P_N\|^{1/d_a}\right).$$ The $d_a$th root measures growth per unit of generator weight, so a monomial of generator degree $D_b$ contributes the power $B_{N+c_b}^{D_b}$. Only $|m|\leq N$ can contribute. The preceding mode expansion gives, for each fixed homogeneous $u\in V_w$, $$\label{eq:rough-monomial-bound}
 \|P_Nu[\mu]P_N\|
 \leq C_u(1+N)^{p_u}B_{N+c_u}^{w}.$$ All cutoffs and exponents in this preliminary estimate are fixed once the expression for $u$ is fixed.

<a id="eq:positive-normal-product"></a>
<a id="eq:positive-column"></a>
We need an initial growth bound before improving <a href="#eq:rough-monomial-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:rough-monomial-bound">[eq:rough-monomial-bound]</a>. For $a\in S$ of weight $d$, set $T_a=(a_{(-1)}a)[0]$. The normal-product formula reads $$\label{eq:positive-normal-product}
 T_a=\sum_{j\geq0}
 \bigl(a[-d-j]a[d+j]+a[d-1-j]a[j+1-d]\bigr).$$ Every summand is a positive square as a finite-energy quadratic form. The second family contains $a[m]^*a[m]$ for every $m\geq0$. Consequently $$\label{eq:positive-column}
 \sum_{m\geq0}\|a[m]\xi\|^2
 \leq \langle T_a\xi,\xi\rangle.$$ Zhu’s ordinary-module one-point convergence theorem applies to $a_{(-1)}a$ <a id="pm-cite-30"></a>[[Zhu](#ref-Zhu), Sections 4.3–4.4 and Theorem 4.4.1]. Its finiteness Condition C holds here: besides $C_2$-cofiniteness and finite-dimensional grades, the required spanning by Virasoro descendants of primary vectors follows from unitarity. For fixed $0<q<1$ we therefore have $$Z_a(q):=\operatorname{Tr}_W(T_aq^{L_0})<\infty.$$ Here the trace is the convergent graded sum $\sum_h q^h\operatorname{Tr}_{W(h)}(T_a|_{W(h)})$; no bounded operator extension of $T_a$ is presumed. Positivity on each energy level and <a href="#eq:positive-column" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:positive-column">[eq:positive-column]</a> give $$\sup_{\substack{\xi\in W(h)\\\|\xi\|=1}}
       \sum_{m\geq0}\|a[m]\xi\|^2
 \leq q^{-h}Z_a(q).$$ Negative compressed modes are controlled by adjunction. It follows that $\log B_N=O(N+1)$. This is the positive-trace method used for the vacuum exponential bound in <a id="pm-cite-31"></a>[[CarpiTomassini](#ref-CarpiTomassini), Lemma 3.5 and Proposition 3.17]; the ordinary-module trace theorem gives the version needed here.

<a id="eq:self-mode-commutator"></a>
<a id="eq:rough-commutator"></a>
For $a\in S$ of weight $d$ and $m>0$, the mode commutator is $$\label{eq:self-mode-commutator}
 C_{a,m}:=[a[m],a[-m]]
   =\sum_{j\geq0}\binom{d-1+m}{j}(a_{(j)}a)[0].$$ The nonzero states on the right have weights at most $2d-1$. Thus <a href="#eq:rough-monomial-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:rough-monomial-bound">[eq:rough-monomial-bound]</a>, uniformly for $1\leq m\leq N$, implies $$\label{eq:rough-commutator}
 \|C_{a,m}P_N\|
 \leq C(1+N)^p B_{N+c}^{2d-1}.$$

<a id="eq:mode-norm-descent"></a>
The descent from a commutator estimate to a mode estimate is the mechanism used in <a id="pm-cite-32"></a>[[CTW](#ref-CTW), Proposition 3.3]. We record it explicitly with its input-energy cutoff, which enters the subsequent iteration. Put $A_h=\|a[-m]:W(h)\longrightarrow W(h+m)\|$, interpreting absent levels as zero. Adjunction and <a href="#eq:self-mode-commutator" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:self-mode-commutator">[eq:self-mode-commutator]</a> give $$\label{eq:mode-norm-descent}
 A_h^2\leq A_{h-m}^2+\|C_{a,m}|_{W(h)}\|.$$ Indeed, apply the identity to a unit vector in $W(h)$, and note that $\|a[m]|_{W(h)}\|=A_{h-m}$. Iterating descends to negative energy in at most $1+N/m$ steps when $h\leq N$. There is no factor depending on the dimensions of the levels. The raising mode may have output above $N$; only the input level enters the commutator estimate.

<a id="eq:virasoro-linear-energy"></a>
<a id="eq:zero-mode-rough-transfer"></a>
The same argument applied to $[L_m,L_{-m}]=2mL_0+\frac c{12}(m^3-m)$ gives $$\label{eq:virasoro-linear-energy}
 \|L_m\xi\|\leq C(1+|m|)^2\|H\xi\|.$$ In particular $L_{\pm1}$ have linear energy bounds. For the zero mode of $a$, use $$\label{eq:zero-mode-rough-transfer}
 d\,a[0]=[L_1,a[-1]].$$ The two products involve only a fixed shift of input energy. Combining <a href="#eq:rough-commutator" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:rough-commutator">[eq:rough-commutator]</a>–<a href="#eq:zero-mode-rough-transfer" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:zero-mode-rough-transfer">[eq:zero-mode-rough-transfer]</a>, and enlarging a common fixed cutoff, yields <a id="eq:first-energy-contraction"></a>$$\label{eq:first-energy-contraction}
 B_N\leq C(1+N)^p B_{N+c}^{\rho},
 \qquad \rho=1-\frac1{2D}<1.$$

The use of an exponential starting bound to close a contraction with an additive cutoff also appears in the proof of <a id="pm-cite-33"></a>[[CarpiTomassini](#ref-CarpiTomassini), Theorem 4.5]. Here the finite-$C_2$ filtration supplies the contracting estimate. The contraction remains effective despite the increasing cutoff: after $r$ iterations, $$\log B_N\leq
 \sum_{j=0}^{r-1}\rho^j
       \bigl(\log C+p\log(1+N+jc)\bigr)
       +\rho^r\log B_{N+rc}.$$ The final term tends to zero by the exponential starting bound. The inequality $1+N+jc\leq(1+N)(1+jc)$ bounds the sum by $C'+\frac p{1-\rho}\log(1+N)$. Hence $B_N$ grows polynomially.

For an arbitrary mode $u[\mu]$ on $W(h)$, apply <a href="#eq:rough-monomial-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:rough-monomial-bound">[eq:rough-monomial-bound]</a> with an endpoint cutoff of size $O(1+h+|\mu|)$. This bounds its norm by a polynomial in $1+h+|\mu|$. Distinct input energy levels have distinct output levels under a fixed mode, so the squared norms add. We have proved polynomial energy bounds for every vertex operator on $W$.

In particular, smooth smears and their formal adjoints map $\mathcal H_W^\infty=\bigcap_{r\geq0}\mathcal D(H^r)$ continuously into itself: the mode shifts cost only polynomial factors, while smooth Fourier coefficients decrease faster than any power. Spectral truncation shows that closing a smear from $W$ or from $\mathcal H_W^\infty$ gives the same operator. These facts will justify the covariance calculation below.

<a id="pm-h-14"></a>
#### A sharper normal-product estimate

Normal-product estimates also prove the closure of polynomial energy bounds under homogeneous generation <a id="pm-cite-34"></a>[[CKLW](#ref-CKLW), Proposition 6.1]. The estimate below keeps the specific energy power needed for the second contraction and locality. We divide the zero-mode norm by its target power $N^{d-1}$ for $d\geq2$. The column normalization is chosen so that its square has the desired energy power $N^{2d-1}$ for the positive zero mode $T_a$. Define $$R_a(N)=\sup_{\substack{\xi\in P_NW\\\|\xi\|\leq1}}
          \left(\sum_{m\geq0}\|a[m]\xi\|^2\right)^{1/2}$$ and, for integer $N\geq1$, <a id="eq:sharp-energy-control"></a>$$\label{eq:sharp-energy-control}
 X_N=\max\left(
 1,
 \max_{a\in S}\left(\frac{R_a(N)}{N^{d_a-1/2}}\right)^{1/d_a},
 \max_{\substack{a\in S\\d_a\geq2}}
       \left(\frac{\|a[0]P_N\|}{N^{d_a-1}}\right)^{1/d_a}
 \right).$$ The preceding bounds show that $X_N$ grows at most polynomially. The column contains at most $1+N$ nonzero modes.

<a id="stmt-lem-sharp-normal-product"></a>
<div id="lem:sharp-normal-product" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:sharp-normal-product"></a>
**Lemma 4**. *For a monomial $b$ in <a href="#eq:energy-monomial" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:energy-monomial">[eq:energy-monomial]</a> of weight $w\geq2$, there are constants $C_b,c_b$ such that $$\label{eq:sharp-normal-product}
 \|b[0]P_N\|
 \leq C_b N^{w-1}X_{N+c_b}^{D_b}\qquad(N\geq1).$$ At weights zero and one the corresponding bounds are a constant and $C_bN^{1/2}X_N$, respectively.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-sharp-normal-product"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-sharp-normal-product</summary>

<a id="eq:left-path-squares"></a>
<a id="eq:right-path-squares"></a>
*Proof.* Use the left/right expansion <a href="#eq:energy-left-right-indices" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:energy-left-right-indices">[eq:energy-left-right-indices]</a> for the zero mode, between unit vectors $\eta,\xi\in P_NW$. Consider first an order pattern having $p\geq1$ left and $q\geq1$ right factors. Let $D_L,D_R$ be the sums of generator weights on those sides, and let $L(\boldsymbol j_L),R(\boldsymbol j_R)$ denote their ordered products of modes. Write $$A_{\boldsymbol j_L}=\|L(\boldsymbol j_L)^*\eta\|,
 \qquad
 B_{\boldsymbol j_R}=\|R(\boldsymbol j_R)\xi\|.$$ Repeated use of the column bound defining $X$, with the fixed intermediate cutoff already established, gives $$\begin{aligned}
 \sum_{\boldsymbol j_L}A_{\boldsymbol j_L}^2
  &\leq C N^{2D_L-p}X_{N+c_b}^{2D_L},\label{eq:left-path-squares}\\
 \sum_{\boldsymbol j_R}B_{\boldsymbol j_R}^2
  &\leq C N^{2D_R-q}X_{N+c_b}^{2D_R}.
       \label{eq:right-path-squares}
\end{aligned}$$ To include the finitely many negative right indices, observe that for a fixed positive integer $s$, $$\|a[-s]P_M\|=\|P_Ma[s]\|
       \leq\|a[s]P_{M+s}\|\leq R_a(M+s).$$ Thus adding those finitely many indices to the column costs only a constant and a fixed cutoff enlargement. Equations <a href="#eq:left-path-squares" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:left-path-squares">[eq:left-path-squares]</a>–<a href="#eq:right-path-squares" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:right-path-squares">[eq:right-path-squares]</a> follow by applying this one-factor estimate successively to the vectors on each path and then summing. Orthogonality between different paths is not needed.

Let $\mathcal L,\mathcal R$ be the sets of left and right factors. By <a href="#eq:energy-left-right-indices" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:energy-left-right-indices">[eq:energy-left-right-indices]</a>, the zero-mode constraint is $$\sum_{i\in\mathcal R}j_i-\sum_{i\in\mathcal L}j_i
   =D_b+\sum_{i\in\mathcal L}n_i-q.$$ Thus one index is determined by the others. Given a left tuple, at most $CN^{q-1}$ right tuples complete it: the last right index is determined by the other $q-1$ indices. Conversely, there are at most $CN^{p-1}$ left completions of a right tuple. Cauchy–Schwarz over the constrained pairs therefore gives $$\begin{aligned}
 \sum_{\text{constraint}}
       A_{\boldsymbol j_L}B_{\boldsymbol j_R}
 &\leq
 \left(CN^{q-1}\sum_{\boldsymbol j_L}A_{\boldsymbol j_L}^2\right)^{1/2}
 \left(CN^{p-1}\sum_{\boldsymbol j_R}B_{\boldsymbol j_R}^2\right)^{1/2}\\
 &\leq C N^{D_L+D_R-1}X_{N+c_b}^{D_L+D_R}.
\end{aligned}$$ The constraint has saved one power of $N$ from the unconstrained count. The binomial coefficients cost at most $N^{\sum_i n_i}$, producing exactly the power $w-1$ in <a href="#eq:sharp-normal-product" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:sharp-normal-product">[eq:sharp-normal-product]</a>.

An all-left pattern cannot have total physical index zero. In an all-right pattern, the indices have fixed lower bounds and sum to zero, so every index and the number of terms are bounded independently of $N$. If $\ell\geq2$, individual column bounds give a power $D_b-\ell/2\leq D_b-1$, which suffices. If $\ell=1$ and $n_1=0$, use the zero-mode term in $X_N$. If $\ell=1$ and $n_1>0$, the weaker power $d_{a^1}-1/2$ is bounded by $w-1$; differentiation at the zero physical mode has a fixed scalar coefficient. The vacuum and weight-one cases follow directly from their defining bounds. ◻

</details>

<a id="eq:sharp-nonzero-mode"></a>
For $a\in S$ of weight $d\geq2$, subtract the identity <a href="#eq:self-mode-commutator" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:self-mode-commutator">[eq:self-mode-commutator]</a> at $m=0$ from that at $m>0$. The former is the zero operator. This removes the $j=0$ term without requiring the state $a_{(0)}a$ to vanish. Only fields of weights at most $2d-2$ remain, with coefficients polynomial in $m$. Lemma <a href="#lem:sharp-normal-product" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:sharp-normal-product">4</a> now gives <a id="eq:sharp-commutator"></a>$$\label{eq:sharp-commutator}
 \|C_{a,m}P_N\|
 \leq C(1+m)^p N^{2d-3}X_{N+c}^{2d-2}.$$ The weight-one estimate also fits this bound because $N^{1/2}\leq N^{2d-3}$; the weight-zero term is bounded. For all other terms the generator degree is at most their conformal weight. The descent <a href="#eq:mode-norm-descent" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:mode-norm-descent">[eq:mode-norm-descent]</a>, with at most $1+N/m$ steps, consequently proves $$\label{eq:sharp-nonzero-mode}
 \|a[m]P_N\|
 \leq C(1+|m|)^pN^{d-1}X_{N+c}^{d-1},
 \qquad m\ne0.$$ For a negative mode this follows directly by applying the raising-mode recurrence at input energy at most $N$. Thus the cutoff in $X$ does not grow with $|m|$. Positive modes of index greater than $N$ vanish.

<a id="pm-h-15"></a>
#### Transferring the bound to the zero mode

The commutator <a href="#eq:zero-mode-rough-transfer" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:zero-mode-rough-transfer">[eq:zero-mode-rough-transfer]</a> would lose one energy power. Möbius covariance transfers the estimate without that loss. For Hermitian primary fields, <a id="pm-cite-35"></a>[[CTW](#ref-CTW), Proposition 3.1] transfers an energy bound from one Fourier mode to all smooth smearings without increasing its energy order. We give the quantitative cutoff transfer for quasi-primary generators because its uniformity is needed in the last iteration.

Let $a\in S$ have weight $d\geq2$. Positive-energy unitary Möbius integration acts on $\mathcal H_W$; it does not require a net representation. With the physical smearing convention, the test $f(e^{i\vartheta})=e^{i\vartheta}$ selects $a[1]$. Write $a(f)=Y^W(a,f)$ for this module smear. The quasi-primary commutators <a href="#eq:energy-adjoint" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:energy-adjoint">[eq:energy-adjoint]</a> integrate on the smooth domain to $$U_g a(f)U_g^*=a(f_g),\qquad
 f_g(g(\vartheta))=g'(\vartheta)^{d-1}f(e^{i\vartheta}).$$ One obtains this identity by differentiating the conjugation with the inversely evolving test; the preliminary polynomial bounds justify the differentiation. The positive-energy Möbius representation and these covariance facts are recalled in <a id="pm-cite-36"></a>[[CTW](#ref-CTW), Section 2].

<a id="eq:mobius-zero-compression"></a>
Take the flow generated by $(L_1-L_{-1})/2$. Its infinitesimal action on $a[1]$ is $$\left[\frac{L_1-L_{-1}}2,a[1]\right]
       =\frac{d-2}{2}a[2]+\frac d2a[0].$$ The mean of $f_g$ therefore has nonzero derivative at the identity. Fix a small $g$ for which that mean, denoted $c_g$, is nonzero. Compression to one energy level gives $$\label{eq:mobius-zero-compression}
 \Pi_hU_ga[1]U_g^*\Pi_h=c_g a[0]\Pi_h.$$

<a id="eq:mobius-tail"></a>
The conjugate $H'=U_gHU_g^*$ is a fixed linear combination of $1,L_0,L_1,L_{-1}$. By <a href="#eq:virasoro-linear-energy" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:virasoro-linear-energy">[eq:virasoro-linear-energy]</a>, $$H'P_MW\subset P_{M+1}W,\qquad
 \|H'P_M\|\leq C_g(1+M).$$ Consequently, for every integer $j\geq1$, <a id="eq:mobius-growing-moment"></a>$$\label{eq:mobius-growing-moment}
 \|H^jU_g^*P_N\|
 =\|(H')^jP_N\|
 \leq C_g^j\prod_{i=0}^{j-1}(1+N+i)
 \leq C_g^j(1+N+j)^j.$$ For fixed powers, the same product estimate gives the usual Sobolev continuity of $U_g$ and $U_g^*$, and hence preservation of $\mathcal H_W^\infty$. For a growing power it controls a spectral tail. Choose an integer $K>4C_g$. If $r$ is fixed and $N>r$, then $$\begin{aligned}
 \|H^r(1-P_{KN})U_g^*P_N\|
 &\leq (KN)^{-(N-r)}\|H^NU_g^*P_N\|\notag\\
 &\leq C_rN^r(3C_g/K)^N.
       \label{eq:mobius-tail}
\end{aligned}$$ The finitely many remaining $N$ are absorbed into the constant. In particular, the tail is bounded in every fixed graph norm, with $K$ independent of $N$ and of the dimension of $P_NW$.

<a id="eq:sharp-zero-mode"></a>
Choose $r$ at least the preliminary polynomial energy order of $a[1]$. Equations <a href="#eq:sharp-nonzero-mode" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:sharp-nonzero-mode">[eq:sharp-nonzero-mode]</a> and <a href="#eq:mobius-tail" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:mobius-tail">[eq:mobius-tail]</a> give $$\begin{aligned}
 \|a[1]U_g^*P_N\|
 &\leq \|a[1]P_{KN}\|
      +\|a[1]H^{-r}\|\,
          \|H^r(1-P_{KN})U_g^*P_N\|\\
 &\leq C N^{d-1}X_{KN+c}^{d-1}+O(1).
\end{aligned}$$ Compress using <a href="#eq:mobius-zero-compression" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:mobius-zero-compression">[eq:mobius-zero-compression]</a>. Since $a[0]$ preserves energy, taking the supremum of its level norms proves $$\label{eq:sharp-zero-mode}
 \|a[0]P_N\|
 \leq C N^{d-1}X_{KN+c}^{d-1}.$$ The bounded error is absorbed because $X\geq1$ and $N\geq1$.

<a id="pm-h-16"></a>
#### Closing the optimal estimate

<a id="eq:sharp-positive-product"></a>
Return to the positive operator $T_a$ in <a href="#eq:positive-normal-product" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:positive-normal-product">[eq:positive-normal-product]</a>, for any generator of weight $d$, including $d=1$. If $2d>D$, its defining state lies in $C_2(V)_{2d}$, so Lemma <a href="#lem:energy-filtration" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:energy-filtration">3</a> supplies an expansion with generator degree at most $2d-1$. If $2d\leq D$, decompose that state into a quasi-primary part and an element of $L_{-1}V_{2d-1}$. The latter is in $C_2(V)$ and has the same strict degree saving. The former is a fixed combination of generators of weight $2d$ and is controlled by <a href="#eq:sharp-zero-mode" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:sharp-zero-mode">[eq:sharp-zero-mode]</a>. In both cases, $$\label{eq:sharp-positive-product}
 \|T_aP_N\|
 \leq C N^{2d-1}X_{K_0N}^{2d-1}$$ for a sufficiently large fixed integer $K_0$.

Here and below the different enlarged cutoffs can indeed be replaced by a common one. Although $X_N$ itself need not be monotone, all its unnormalized column and zero-mode numerators are monotone. If $N\leq M\leq cN$, its definition gives $X_N\leq cX_M$. The finitely many fixed cutoff enlargements therefore change only the constant in <a href="#eq:sharp-positive-product" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:sharp-positive-product">[eq:sharp-positive-product]</a>.

Take the square root in <a href="#eq:positive-column" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:positive-column">[eq:positive-column]</a> and <a href="#eq:sharp-positive-product" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:sharp-positive-product">[eq:sharp-positive-product]</a>, and then the $d$th root in the definition of $X_N$. The column term contracts with exponent $(2d-1)/(2d)$. Equation <a href="#eq:sharp-zero-mode" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:sharp-zero-mode">[eq:sharp-zero-mode]</a> contracts each zero-mode term with exponent $(d-1)/d$. Thus <a id="eq:second-energy-contraction"></a>$$\label{eq:second-energy-contraction}
 X_N\leq C X_{K_0N}^{\sigma},\qquad
 \sigma=1-\frac1{2D}<1.$$ The first contraction allowed an additive cutoff enlargement and used an exponential starting bound. Here the cutoff grows multiplicatively; the polynomial bound already proved for $X_N$ suffices. Namely, $$\log X_N\leq\frac{\log C}{1-\sigma}
        +\sigma^j\log X_{K_0^jN},$$ and the last term is bounded by $\sigma^j(C'+s\log N+sj\log K_0)$, which tends to zero. We conclude that $X_N$ is uniformly bounded.

Equations <a href="#eq:sharp-nonzero-mode" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:sharp-nonzero-mode">[eq:sharp-nonzero-mode]</a> and <a href="#eq:sharp-zero-mode" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:sharp-zero-mode">[eq:sharp-zero-mode]</a> now give <a href="#eq:optimal-vertex-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:optimal-vertex-bound">[eq:optimal-vertex-bound]</a> for every $a\in S$ of weight at least two. To pass from cutoff estimates to a general finite-energy vector, use $N=\max(1,\lceil h\rceil)$ on each input level $W(h)$ and add squared norms: a fixed mode has different output energies for different input energies. Any specified Hermitian quasi-primary can be included in such an $S$ by enlarging the fixed integer $D$.

For a weight-one generator, boundedness of $X_N$ gives $R_a(N)\leq CN^{1/2}$. For positive modes this is already a half-order energy estimate. For a negative mode $-m$, adjunction on the output level gives a bound by $C(1+h+m)^{1/2}$ on $W(h)$, and $$(1+h+m)^{1/2}\leq(1+m)^{1/2}(1+h)^{1/2}.$$ Thus weight-one fields satisfy, in particular, at most linear energy bounds. Linear combinations of the Hermitian generators give the same conclusion for all of $V_1$. The Virasoro estimate was proved in <a href="#eq:virasoro-linear-energy" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:virasoro-linear-energy">[eq:virasoro-linear-energy]</a>. This completes the proof of Theorem <a href="#thm:energy" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:energy">2</a>.

<a id="stmt-cor-locality"></a>
<div id="cor:locality" class="corollary">

**Corollary 5**. *Every simple unitary strongly rational VOA $V$ is CKLW-strongly local. Its smeared vertex operators generate an irreducible conformal net $A_V$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-cor-locality"><summary>Proof of stmt-cor-locality</summary>

*Proof.* The unitary Virasoro decomposition expresses $V$ as a sum of highest-weight representations. Equivalently, at each energy the orthogonal complement of the negative-Virasoro-mode descendants consists of primary vectors. These primary spaces are spanned by Hermitian vectors. Hence the conformal vector and Hermitian primary vectors generate $V$; see <a id="pm-cite-37"></a>[[CTW](#ref-CTW), Remark 3.9]. A finite family suffices: decompose the vectors in $S$ into Virasoro descendants and take Hermitian bases of the finitely many primary spaces that occur. Together with $\nu$, these vectors generate $S$ and hence $V$.

Theorem <a href="#thm:energy" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:energy">2</a> gives the optimal order $d-1$ for every such primary of weight $d\geq2$, and supplies the required current and Virasoro bounds. The corrected strong-locality criterion <a id="pm-cite-38"></a>[[CTW](#ref-CTW), Theorem 3.7], with its Hermitian hypothesis <a id="pm-cite-39"></a>[[CTWCorrection](#ref-CTWCorrection)], therefore applies. Finally <a id="pm-cite-40"></a>[[CKLW](#ref-CKLW), Theorem 6.8] constructs the irreducible conformal net from this simple unitary strongly local VOA. ◻

</details>

We have constructed $A_V$ independently of any fusion positivity or module integration. In the following section it is this vacuum net that supplies the annulus operators.

<a id="sec:annuli"></a>
### Annuli and charged-field estimates

We now use the conformal net $A_V$ constructed in Corollary <a href="#cor:locality" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cor:locality">5</a>. The purpose of this section is to obtain analytic bounds for charged fields before constructing the net representations associated to their target modules. The source of a charged field will initially be the vacuum module $V$. More generally, the argument works whenever its source module has already been integrated. This order will allow us to prove integration before using positivity of general fusion products.

<a id="pm-h-18"></a>
#### Localized annuli and their ranges

We use the annulus constructions of Henriques–Tener <a id="pm-cite-41"></a>[[HTVirasoro](#ref-HTVirasoro), [HTVOA](#ref-HTVOA), [HTGenusZero](#ref-HTGenusZero)]. An annulus may have incoming and outgoing boundaries that touch, but do not cross. A lift of an annulus means an element of the centrally extended covering semigroup $\widetilde{\operatorname{Ann}}_c$, where $c$ is the central charge. Lifts act by bounded operators in positive-energy unitary Virasoro representations. Gluing is multiplication, with the right factor acting first, and reflection is denoted by $\dagger$ and acts by the Hilbert-space adjoint. On the grading-restricted unitary modules used below, fixed lifted annuli and their adjoints preserve every energy Sobolev space and hence the smooth domain continuously <a id="pm-cite-42"></a>[[HTVirasoro](#ref-HTVirasoro), Theorem 4.8, Remark 4.9 and Theorem 5.5].

The VOA reconstructed from $A_V$ is $V$ itself <a id="pm-cite-43"></a>[[HTVOA](#ref-HTVOA), Theorem 8.9(ii)]. Thus the point-insertion labels in that construction are the vectors of $V$. We write $\underline A[v(z)]$ for an insertion with the standard local coordinate $\zeta\mapsto z+\zeta$. More generally, $p_*v(0)$ denotes the label obtained by a change of local coordinate $p$; for $p(\zeta)=z+q\zeta$, $q>0$, it is $(q^{L_0}v)(z)$. Finite-energy labels are transported by the usual formal coordinate action. Insertions compose under gluing and depend holomorphically on their positions. They also commute with reflection <a id="pm-cite-44"></a>[[HTVOA](#ref-HTVOA), Section 6 and Proposition 6.19].

For $0<\rho\leq1$ a smooth function on $S^1$, consider the radial annulus whose incoming and outgoing parametrizations are $$e^{i\vartheta}\longmapsto\rho(e^{i\vartheta})e^{i\vartheta},
 \qquad e^{i\vartheta}\longmapsto e^{i\vartheta}.$$ If $1-\rho$ is compactly supported in an interval $I$, radial interpolation gives a lift $\underline A$ localized in $I$. Its vacuum operator belongs to $A_V(I)$. The same holds for its point insertions, and their actions in a locally normal representation $\pi$ are their images under $\pi_I$ <a id="pm-cite-45"></a>[[HTVOA](#ref-HTVOA), Lemma 6.8]. The reflected double $$\widehat A=\underline A^\dagger\underline A$$ can be drawn in the plane between the radial curves $\rho$ and $\rho^{-1}$. The two annuli meet along the unit circle.

The complementary-annulus argument below is the exact-preimage argument of <a id="pm-cite-46"></a>[[HTVOA](#ref-HTVOA), Lemma 7.9]. We record its range and density consequences in the form used for charged fields.

<a id="stmt-lem-annulus-range"></a>
<div id="lem:annulus-range" class="lemma">

**Lemma 6** (Ranges of annulus operators). *Let $\underline A,\underline B$ be lifted annuli satisfying $\underline A\underline B=s_0^{L_0}$, with $0<s_0<1$, in a positive-energy representation with dense finite-energy subspace $E$. Then $$E\subset\operatorname{ran}\underline A,
 \qquad \underline A^{-1}E=\underline B E,
 \qquad \overline{\underline A^{-1}E}=\mathcal H.$$ Every radial annulus above admits such a complementary factor $\underline B$ when $s_0<\min\rho$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-annulus-range"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-annulus-range</summary>

*Proof.* The geometric complement between the circle of radius $s_0$ and the incoming boundary gives $B$. Choose its lift so that the product is the standard lift of the round annulus; any necessary scalar and winding are absorbed in this fixed choice. Annulus operators are injective with dense range <a id="pm-cite-47"></a>[[HTVOA](#ref-HTVOA), Lemma 4.15]. Since $s_0^{L_0}$ is invertible on $E$, its range contains $E$, and $\underline B E\subset
\underline A^{-1}E$. Conversely, if $\underline A\xi\in E$, injectivity of $\underline A$ gives $$\xi=\underline B s_0^{-L_0}\underline A\xi\in\underline B E.$$ Finally, $\underline B$ is bounded with dense range and $E$ is dense. These observations also prove the assertion when the energy eigenspaces are not finite dimensional; compare <a id="pm-cite-48"></a>[[HTVOA](#ref-HTVOA), Lemma 7.9]. ◻

</details>

<a id="eq:round-insertion"></a>
<a id="eq:annulus-insertion"></a>
Let $N$ be an already strongly integrable unitary $V$-module, with local representations $\pi_I^N$. Subscripts $N$ on annulus operators refer to the resulting net representation. The Virasoro action used here is the module Virasoro action: the closed stress-energy smears agree by strong integration, and a partition of unity identifies their modes, including $L_0$. The reconstructed module is consequently $N$, by the uniqueness statement in <a id="pm-cite-49"></a>[[HTVOA](#ref-HTVOA), Theorem 9.5(ii) and Corollary 9.6]. In particular, for the standard lifted round annulus with radii $s<R$, $$\label{eq:round-insertion}
 A_{R,s,N}[v(z)]=R^{-L_0}Y^N(v,z)s^{L_0}$$ in finite-energy matrix coefficients. For a radial annulus $A$ and $z$ in its interior, gluing with the complementary factor in Lemma <a href="#lem:annulus-range" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:annulus-range">6</a> gives $$\label{eq:annulus-insertion}
 \begin{split}
 \underline A_N[v(z)]\xi
   &=Y^N(v,z)\underline A_N\xi,\\
 \langle\widehat A_N[v(z)]\xi,\eta\rangle
   &=\langle Y^N(v,z)\underline A_N\xi,
                         \underline A_N\eta\rangle,
 \qquad \xi,\eta\in\underline A_N^{-1}N.
 \end{split}$$ These are identities on the indicated exact dense preimages, rather than identities of arbitrary products of unbounded operators.

<a id="pm-h-19"></a>
#### A fixed-disc estimate and a uniform round estimate

<a id="stmt-lem-fixed-disc"></a>
<div id="lem:fixed-disc" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:fixed-disc-bound"></a>
**Lemma 7** (Insertion in a fixed disc). *Let $\widehat A=\underline A^\dagger\underline A$ be a fixed localized double, and let $S_0$ be a closed round disc strictly inside it. There is a constant $C_{\widehat A,S_0}$ such that, for every Möbius embedding $p:\mathbb D\to\mathring S_0$ and $v\in V$, $$\label{eq:fixed-disc-bound}
 \|\widehat A_N[p_*v(0)]\|
     \leq C_{\widehat A,S_0}\|v\|$$ in every already integrated unitary module $N$. Here $\mathbb D$ is the closed unit disc, and embeddings are smooth up to their boundaries.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-fixed-disc"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-fixed-disc</summary>

*Proof.* Write the underlying double as $D_{\rm out}\setminus
\mathring D_{\rm in}$. The inclusion $S_0\sqcup D_{\rm in}\to D_{\rm out}$ is a multidisc embedding: the incoming closed discs are disjoint, although $D_{\rm in}$ may meet the outgoing boundary. The bounded map of <a id="pm-cite-50"></a>[[HTGenusZero](#ref-HTGenusZero), Theorem A, or Theorem 3.6] yields $$H_0(S_0)\otimes H_0(D_{\rm in})\longrightarrow H_0(D_{\rm out}).$$ Evaluating its first slot at an inserted vector, and using <a id="pm-cite-51"></a>[[HTGenusZero](#ref-HTGenusZero), Corollary A.1], gives the corresponding inserted annulus operator. The boundary identification operators are unitary; the scalar comparing the fixed lift with this vacuum map is independent of the insertion <a id="pm-cite-52"></a>[[HTVOA](#ref-HTVOA), Lemma 6.10].

Identify $S_0$ conformally with $\mathbb D$. The resulting Möbius embedding factors into disc automorphisms and a dilation of radius less than one. Its covered univalent lift acts by unitaries and a positive-energy contraction. The state associated to $p_*v(0)$ in $H_0(S_0)$ therefore has norm at most $\|v\|$. This proves the vacuum bound. The inserted vacuum operator belongs to $A_V(I)$ for the localizing interval of the double. Its image under $\pi_I^N$ is the inserted operator on $N$; contractivity proves the same bound there. ◻

</details>

In particular, over a compact subarc on which $\rho<1$, put $$\delta=|z|^{-1}-|z|,\qquad q=2\delta.$$ For $|z|<1$ sufficiently close to one, the small disc $z+q\mathbb D$ lies in one of finitely many fixed discs strictly inside the double. Lemma <a href="#lem:fixed-disc" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:fixed-disc">7</a> is thus uniform for the labels $(q^{L_0}v)(z)$ throughout that collar. The next estimate supplies the corresponding uniform control when both boundaries are round and shrink towards the insertion. A bound for each fixed annulus would not control this degeneration. We instead factor the round cutoff through one fixed dented annulus: the moving factors will be a Möbius unitary and a contraction, while the pulled-back insertion stays in one fixed disc.

<a id="stmt-prop-round-annulus"></a>
<div id="prop:round-annulus" class="proposition">

<a id="eq:uniform-round"></a>
<a id="eq:round-comparable"></a>
**Proposition 8** (Uniform round-annulus estimate). *Let $N$ be an already integrated unitary $V$-module. Set $$a=1-10^{-4},\qquad
 \gamma_t(\zeta)=\frac{\zeta+t}{1+t\zeta},\qquad
 r=\gamma_t(a),\qquad s=\gamma_t(1/2),\qquad 0\leq t<1.$$ With $\delta=r^{-1}-r$ and $q=2\delta$, there is a constant $C_N$, independent of $t$ and $v$, such that $$\label{eq:uniform-round}
 \|A_{1/s,s,N}[(q^{L_0}v)(r)]\|\leq C_N\|v\|,
 \qquad v\in V.$$ Moreover, $$\label{eq:round-comparable}
 5000\leq\frac{1-s}{1-r}\leq6667.$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-prop-round-annulus"><summary>Proof of stmt-prop-round-annulus</summary>

*Proof.* *The fixed geometry.* Fix $p_0(\zeta)=-0.2+0.8\zeta$, whose image disc has diameter $[-1,0.6]$. Choose a radial annulus $A_*$ with inner radius $0.85$ for $|\vartheta|\leq0.1$, inner radius between $0.85$ and $1$ everywhere, and inner radius $1$ for $|\vartheta|\geq0.2$. Its incoming disc contains $p_0(\mathbb D)$: over $|\vartheta|<0.2$ the latter disc has radial boundary less than $0.61$, and elsewhere it lies in $\mathbb D$. The double of $A_*$ contains $$S_0=\{\zeta:|\zeta-1|\leq0.05\}$$ strictly in its interior, since this disc has argument of absolute value less than $0.051$ and radii between $0.95$ and $1.05$. Factor the univalent annulus $p_0$ as $A_*C$, and fix lifts with $\underline A_*\underline C$ equal to the covered univalent lift of $p_0$. The factor $C$ is fixed; Figure <a href="#fig:fixed-annulus" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="fig:fixed-annulus">1</a> shows the geometry of its outer factor.

<figure id="fig:fixed-annulus">

<figcaption>The fixed geometry used in the round-annulus factorization. The blue disc $p_{0}(𝔻)$ lies in the incoming disc, and the orange insertion disc lies strictly inside the gray doubled annulus. The dashed curve is the unit-circle interface. The transition curves in the overview are schematic; the magnified panel uses the stated radii on the central part of the dent.</figcaption>
</figure>



*Factoring the shrinking annulus.* The map $\gamma_t$ moves the fixed point $a$ to $r$. We arrange the remaining incoming boundary by writing $s\operatorname{id}=\gamma_t\circ p_0\circ b_t$, namely by defining $$b_t=p_0^{-1}\circ\gamma_t^{-1}\circ(s\operatorname{id}).$$ After doubling, the factor $\gamma_t$ will cancel against its adjoint. The insertion will then lie between the same fixed factors $\underline C^*$ and $\underline C$, with only the contractions $\underline b_t^*$ and $\underline b_t$ outside them.

<a id="eq:round-factorization"></a>
The disc $\gamma_t^{-1}(s\mathbb D)$ has real diameter with endpoints $-(s+t)/(1+st)>-1$ and $1/2$. It lies strictly in $p_0(\mathbb D)$, so $b_t$ is a strict Möbius disc embedding. Its covered univalent lift is a contraction, whereas the lift of $\gamma_t$ is unitary. Choose these lifts so that $$\label{eq:round-factorization}
 s^{L_0}=\underline\gamma_t\,
          \underline A_*\underline C\,\underline b_t$$ is the standard round-annulus lift. This is an equality in the covered univalent semigroup, whose inclusion lifts to $\widetilde{\operatorname{Ann}}_c$ <a id="pm-cite-53"></a>[[HTVOA](#ref-HTVOA), Section 4.2]: the Virasoro cocycle vanishes on the univalent subalgebra, so this lift introduces no scalar ambiguity. Any winding adjustment acts unitarily.

Consider the insertion coordinate $p_t(\zeta)=\gamma_t^{-1}(r+q\zeta)$. The identities $$1-tr=\frac{1-t^2}{1+ta},\qquad
 \frac{q}{1-tr}=\frac{2(1-a^2)}{t+a}<0.000401,$$ and $$\gamma_t^{-1}(r+h)-a
   =\frac{(1-t^2)h}{(1-tr)(1-tr-th)}$$ show, for $|h|\leq q$, that $$|\gamma_t^{-1}(r+h)-a|
  \leq\frac{2(0.000401)}{1-0.000401}<0.000803.$$ Consequently $p_t(\mathbb D)\subset\mathring S_0$ for every $t$. The map has no pole there. Also $s>t$, so the pole $1/t$ of $\gamma_t^{-1}$ is outside the round annulus with outer radius $1/s$. Since $\gamma_t$ commutes with reflection in the unit circle, we may double <a href="#eq:round-factorization" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:round-factorization">[eq:round-factorization]</a>, carrying along the insertion. The unitary factors cancel, and gluing gives the exact operator identity <a id="eq:round-doubled"></a>$$\label{eq:round-doubled}
 A_{1/s,s,N}[(q^{L_0}v)(r)]
 =\underline b_{t,N}^{*}\underline C_N^{*}
    (\underline A_*^\dagger\underline A_*)_N[(p_t)_*v(0)]
    \underline C_N\underline b_{t,N}.$$ *The uniform bound.* Lemma <a href="#lem:fixed-disc" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:fixed-disc">7</a> bounds the middle factor by a fixed constant times $\|v\|$. Thus the right side has norm at most $C_{\widehat A_*,S_0}\|\underline C_N\|^2\|v\|$. In particular, no radius-dependent comparison scalar is introduced.

Finally, $$\frac{1-s}{1-r}
 =5000\frac{1+ta}{1+t/2},$$ which proves <a href="#eq:round-comparable" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:round-comparable">[eq:round-comparable]</a>. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-20"></a>
#### Transport forms and infinite insertion states

<a id="eq:transport-form"></a>
Recall from <a href="#eq:creation" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:creation">[eq:creation]</a> that, for a unitary module $M$, the creation and annihilation intertwiners are $$\mathcal Y_M^+(a,z)v=e^{zL_{-1}}Y^M(v,-z)a,
 \qquad \mathcal Y_M^-=(\mathcal Y_M^+)^\dagger.$$ Their types are respectively $\binom{M}{M\,V}$ and $\binom{V}{\bar M\,M}$. The charge transform <a href="#eq:theta-convention" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:theta-convention">[eq:theta-convention]</a>, for a complex coordinate with a chosen logarithm, is $$\theta_{\bar z}a
   =e^{\bar zL_1}e^{i\pi L_0}\bar z^{-2L_0}\bar a,$$ using the conjugate logarithm at $\bar z$. For unitary modules $M,N$, define the diagonal transport form by $$\label{eq:transport-form}
 \mathfrak t_z^{M,N}(a\otimes b,a'\otimes b')
 =\big\langle
 Y^N\big(\mathcal Y_M^-(\theta_{\bar z}a',
                         \bar z^{-1}-z)a,z\big)b,b'\big\rangle.$$ We use this for $0<|z|<1$ and $|z|>|z|^{-1}-|z|$. At positive real $z$ all positive-coordinate logarithms are real; on a rotated diagonal the two insertion coordinates have the same argument and the reflected logarithm is the conjugate one. The form is linear in its first entry.

<a id="eq:creation-transport"></a>
Regularity gives absolute convergence of these matrix coefficients. More precisely, the ordinary intertwiners between unitary modules have Hilbert-space values at $0<|z|<1$, with branchwise norm holomorphy <a id="pm-cite-54"></a>[[TenerFusion](#ref-TenerFusion), Definition 2.8, Lemma 2.9 and Proposition 3.11]. Creation satisfies the identity $$\label{eq:creation-transport}
 \mathfrak t_z^{M,V}(a\otimes b,a'\otimes b')
   =\langle\mathcal Y_M^+(a,z)b,
                  \mathcal Y_M^+(a',z)b'\rangle$$ without assuming energy bounds, integration of $M$, or positivity of general fusion forms <a id="pm-cite-55"></a>[[TenerFusion](#ref-TenerFusion), Proposition 3.10].

<a id="stmt-lem-transport-cutoff"></a>
<div id="lem:transport-cutoff" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:annulus-hilbert-state"></a>
<a id="eq:transport-cutoff"></a>
**Lemma 9** (Hilbert-state insertions). *Let $M,N$ be unitary modules, with $N$ already integrated. For homogeneous $a,a'\in M$ of weights $d,d'$, set $$u_z=\mathcal Y_M^-(\theta_{\bar z}a',\bar z^{-1}-z)a,
 \qquad \delta=|z|^{-1}-|z|,\qquad q=2\delta.$$ As $|z|$ tends to one on a fixed branch, $$\label{eq:annulus-hilbert-state}
 q^{-L_0}u_z\in\mathcal H_V,
 \qquad \|q^{-L_0}u_z\|\leq C_{a,a'}\delta^{-d-d'}.$$ For a fixed dent with $z$ in its inner collar, $\widehat A_N[u_z(z)]$ is well defined by norm limits of energy truncations, belongs to $\pi_I^N(A_V(I))$, and has norm at most $C\delta^{-d-d'}$. On $\underline A_N^{-1}N$ in both variables it obeys $$\label{eq:transport-cutoff}
 \langle\widehat A_N[u_z(z)]\xi,\eta\rangle
 =\mathfrak t_z^{M,N}
   (a\otimes\underline A_N\xi,
    a'\otimes\underline A_N\eta).$$ For the round annuli of Proposition <a href="#prop:round-annulus" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:round-annulus">8</a>, the same bound holds with the cutoffs $s^{L_0}$ in both entries and $z=r$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-transport-cutoff"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-transport-cutoff</summary>

*Proof.* Expand $\theta_{\bar z}a'$ into finitely many vectors of weights $d_j\leq d'$. Their coefficients are bounded as $|z|$ tends to one. By $L_0$-covariance, applying $q^{-L_0}$ to each annihilation value produces the factor $q^{-d_j-d}$ and an annihilation value at modulus $\delta/q=1/2$. The latter is a Hilbert-space vector by <a id="pm-cite-56"></a>[[TenerFusion](#ref-TenerFusion), Proposition 3.11]; rotations preserve its norm. This proves <a href="#eq:annulus-hilbert-state" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:annulus-hilbert-state">[eq:annulus-hilbert-state]</a>.

Let $P_n$ be the vacuum energy cutoff. Apply the uniform collar version of <a href="#eq:fixed-disc-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:fixed-disc-bound">[eq:fixed-disc-bound]</a> to $q^{-L_0}(P_n-P_m)u_z$. It gives $$\|\widehat A_N[(P_n u_z)(z)]
       -\widehat A_N[(P_m u_z)(z)]\|
 \leq C\|q^{-L_0}(P_n-P_m)u_z\|.$$ Thus these operators converge in norm, with the asserted bound. Locality and representation transfer persist under that norm limit. For finite truncations, <a href="#eq:annulus-insertion" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:annulus-insertion">[eq:annulus-insertion]</a> applies. Absolute convergence of the transport iterate identifies its limiting matrix coefficients on the exact dense preimages, proving <a href="#eq:transport-cutoff" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:transport-cutoff">[eq:transport-cutoff]</a>. The identical argument using <a href="#eq:uniform-round" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:uniform-round">[eq:uniform-round]</a> and <a href="#eq:round-insertion" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:round-insertion">[eq:round-insertion]</a> gives the round-annulus assertion. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-21"></a>
#### Charged modes and bounded boundary smears

<a id="stmt-lem-charged-tools"></a>
<div id="lem:charged-tools" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:transport-domination"></a>
**Lemma 10** (Analytic tools for a dominated intertwiner). *Let $M,N,K$ be grading-restricted unitary $V$-modules, with $N$ already strongly integrable, and let $\Phi$ have type $\binom{K}{M\,N}$. Fix a homogeneous $a\in M$ of weight $d$. Suppose that, for all finite-energy $b\in N$ and all diagonal positions sufficiently close to the unit circle on the branches under consideration, $$\label{eq:transport-domination}
 \|\Phi(a,z)b\|^2
   \leq C_a\mathfrak t_z^{M,N}(a\otimes b,a\otimes b).$$ Then $\Phi(a,z)$ has polynomial energy bounds, as do its levelwise adjoint coefficients. Its smooth smears and their formal adjoints preserve the smooth domains continuously.*

<a id="eq:charged-bounded-cutoff"></a>
*For every smooth test $f$ supported compactly in an interval $I$ with a chosen argument, there is a localized radial annulus $\underline A$ with a dent over $\operatorname{supp}f$ such that $$\label{eq:charged-bounded-cutoff}
 T_f=\overline{\Phi(a,f)}\,\underline A_N
       :\mathcal H_N\longrightarrow\mathcal H_K$$ is bounded and defined on all of $\mathcal H_N$. Both $T_f$ and $T_f^*$ preserve smooth vectors in their respective directions. Moreover, $$N\subset\operatorname{ran}\underline A_N,
 \qquad \underline A_N^{-1}N\text{ is dense in }\mathcal H_N.$$ These conclusions apply to $\Phi=\mathcal Y_M^+$ and $N=V$ for every already unitary $M$, before integrating $M$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-charged-tools"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-charged-tools</summary>

<a id="eq:charged-radial-estimate"></a>
<a id="eq:charged-mode-estimate"></a>
*Proof.* Lemma <a href="#lem:transport-cutoff" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:transport-cutoff">9</a> and Proposition <a href="#prop:round-annulus" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:round-annulus">8</a> give $$\label{eq:charged-radial-estimate}
 \|\Phi(a,r)s^{L_0}b\|
      \leq C(1-r)^{-d}\|b\|,
 \qquad b\in N.$$ Write the physical expansion as $\Phi(a,z)=\sum_\mu\Phi(a)[\mu]z^{-\mu-d}$, so that a coefficient maps energy $h$ to $h-\mu$. For $b$ of energy $h$, project <a href="#eq:charged-radial-estimate" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:charged-radial-estimate">[eq:charged-radial-estimate]</a> to that output energy. Fix $0<\varepsilon_a\leq10^{-4}$ small enough that the transport domination and the cutoff estimate hold whenever $1-\varepsilon_a\leq r<1$, and choose $$1-r=\frac{\varepsilon_a}{1+h+|\mu|}.$$ The corresponding $t$ lies in $[0,1)$. By <a href="#eq:round-comparable" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:round-comparable">[eq:round-comparable]</a>, $s\geq1/2$ and $1-s\leq(6667)10^{-4}/(1+h+|\mu|)$. Since $-\log s\leq2(1-s)$, the factor $s^{-h}$ is uniformly bounded. The factor $r^{\mu+d}$ is uniformly bounded as well, using $d\geq0$ and $r\geq1-10^{-4}$. Hence $$\label{eq:charged-mode-estimate}
 \|\Phi(a)[\mu]b\|
     \leq C(1+h+|\mu|)^d\|b\|.$$ Distinct input energies have distinct output energies for a fixed coefficient. Orthogonality therefore turns this into a polynomial energy bound on $N$. Taking adjoints between individual energy spaces gives the same kind of bound for the adjoint coefficients. Choose any integer energy order at least $d$ when an integer order is desired.

On a fixed argument interval, define $\Phi(a,f)$ by smearing its physical field $z^d\Phi(a,z)$ against $f(e^{i\vartheta})\,d\vartheta/(2\pi)$. Other smooth weight factors can be absorbed into $f$. Rapid decay of Fourier coefficients, <a href="#eq:charged-mode-estimate" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:charged-mode-estimate">[eq:charged-mode-estimate]</a>, and the energy shifts show that this smear and its formal adjoint map smooth vectors continuously to smooth vectors. For clarity, a target Sobolev seminorm of order $j$ is bounded by an input seminorm of a fixed higher order, times a summable series of polynomially weighted Fourier coefficients.
